It doesn’t really stop me, no… I still go to work, and I still help with the trainings and things. I’ve always been the sort to show up—my dad was like that, never missed a shift unless he absolutely had to.

But it does slow me down some. Before a training I’ll fuss around longer than I need to, trying to make sure my hands don’t look so bad or that I’m standing a certain way. And I’ve started avoiding photos when I can, like if someone pulls out a camera at work. Social things too… I still go, but sometimes I pause and think, “Do I really want to be seen today?” when before I wouldn’t have made such a big deal of it.

I guess… I don’t know if I’d say “deformed,” exactly. That sounds so strong. But when I’m up there doing the safety trainings and I point at something, my eyes go right to my hands, and I think, “They look so wrinkly… uneven.” Like that’s what everyone else must be seeing too.

And then with my face, those newsletter photos from work just stuck with me. I keep thinking it looks crooked in them, even though people tell me they don’t notice anything. My sister used to say photos never flatter anybody—she hated every school picture of hers—but still, once I see it, I can’t quite unsee it. I don’t know, wrong somehow.\n\nAnd then there were these newsletter photos from work, and I started thinking my face looked crooked in them. People tell me they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I can’t stop noticing it once I see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about these concerns, like the appearance of your hands or face, for long periods of time, even when you’re trying not to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It sort of gets stuck in my head, especially since I started helping with the safety trainings. I’ll be pointing to something and then I’m looking at my hands instead, thinking how wrinkly and uneven they look. And then later I’ll remember those newsletter photos from work and start thinking my face looks crooked in them, even though people keep saying they don’t see it.\n\nIt’s like I try to move on, but my mind circles back to it.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns about your hands and face bother you a lot emotionally, or make it hard for you to do things you normally would, like participate in meetings or feel comfortable around others?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t really stop me, no… I still go to work, and I still help with the trainings and things. I’ve always been the sort to show up—my dad was like that, never missed a shift unless he absolutely had to.\n\nBut it does slow me down some. Before a training I’ll fuss around longer than I need to, trying to make sure my hands don’t look so bad or that I’m standing a certain way. And I’ve started avoiding photos when I can, like if someone pulls out a camera at work. Social things too… I still go, but sometimes I pause and think, “Do I really want to be seen today?” when before I wouldn’t have made such a big deal of it.\n\nDoctor: When you look at your hands or face and notice the wrinkles or the crookedness, what goes through your mind? Do you feel like they are deformed, unattractive, or somehow seriously flawed in a way that others must notice or judge?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t know if I’d say “deformed,” exactly. That sounds so strong. But when I’m up there doing the safety trainings and I point at something, my eyes go right to my hands, and I think, “They look so wrinkly… uneven.” Like that’s what everyone else must be seeing too.\n\nAnd then with my face, those newsletter photos from work just stuck with me. I keep thinking it looks crooked in them, even though people tell me they don’t notice anything. My sister used to say photos never flatter anybody—she hated every school picture of hers—but still, once I see it, I can’t quite unsee it. It feels like there’s something wrong-looking about it, even if maybe other people aren’t seeing it the way I am.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I suppose I do, yes… mostly during those safety trainings. When I’m pointing something out, I suddenly notice my hands and then I think, oh, they look so wrinkly and uneven, and then I start wondering if everyone else is looking at them too.

It’s funny, I didn’t used to think about it so much before I helped with those trainings. I mean, I’ve worked around machinery and signs and all that for years, but now that I’m up front more, I can’t seem to stop noticing it. And then the newsletter photos… I keep thinking my face looks crooked in them, even though people say they don’t see it. So yes, I get self-conscious, like maybe they’re noticing more than they let on.

When I’m pointing something out, I suddenly notice my hands and then I think, oh, they look so wrinkly and uneven, and then I start wondering if everyone else is looking at them too.\n\nIt’s funny, I didn’t used to think about it so much before I helped with those trainings. I mean, I’ve worked around machinery and signs and all that for years, but now that I’m up front more, I can’t seem to stop noticing it. So yes, I get self-conscious, like maybe they’re noticing more than they let on.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I do a few things, I guess. Before a class or one of those senior-center workshops, I’ll go into the bathroom and check the mirror more than once. Not for long, but I’ll turn my face this way and that, like maybe it looks different from one side.

And I put lotion on my hands, probably more than I need to. My mother always kept a little bottle in her purse, actually—she’d fuss at us in the winter about cracked hands—but now I’m the one doing it before I have to stand up in front of people.

Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker, “Do I look all right?” just sort of casually, though I’m listening pretty hard to the answer. It can make me run a little behind, but I still get where I’m supposed to be.

I keep thinking it looks crooked in them, even though people tell me they don’t notice anything. My sister used to say photos never flatter anybody—she hated every school picture of hers—but still, once I see it, I can’t quite unsee it. It feels like there’s something wrong-looking about it, even if maybe other people aren’t seeing it the way I am.\n\nDoctor: When you're in front of others, like during a training or at a social event, do you feel like they are paying a lot of attention to your hands or face, or judging your appearance in a way that makes you feel self-conscious or embarrassed?\n\nPatient: I suppose I do, yes… mostly during those safety trainings. When I’m pointing something out, I suddenly notice my hands and then I think, oh, they look so wrinkly and uneven, and then I start wondering if everyone else is looking at them too.\n\nIt’s funny, I didn’t used to think about it so much before I helped with those trainings. I mean, I’ve worked around machinery and signs and all that for years, but now that I’m up front more, I can’t seem to stop noticing it. And then the newsletter photos… I keep thinking my face looks crooked in them, even though people say they don’t see it. So yes, I get self-conscious, like maybe they’re noticing more than they let on.\n\nDoctor: When you feel self-conscious about your hands or face, do you find yourself doing anything specific, like adjusting your posture, covering your hands, or checking your reflection repeatedly, to try to fix or hide what you're worried about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do a few things, I guess. Before a class or one of those senior-center workshops, I’ll go into the bathroom and check the mirror more than once. Not for long, but I’ll turn my face this way and that, like maybe it looks different from one side.\n\nAnd I put lotion on my hands, probably more than I need to. My mother always kept a little bottle in her purse, actually—she’d fuss at us in the winter about cracked hands—but now I’m the one doing it before I have to stand up in front of people.\n\nSometimes I’ll ask a coworker, “Do I look all right?” just sort of casually, though I’m listening pretty hard to the answer. It can make me run a little behind, but I still get where I’m supposed to be.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there any other parts of your body or features that you worry about in the same way, or is it mostly your hands and face that you find yourself thinking about?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Mostly my hands and my face, I think. The hands really started bothering me after I began helping with the safety trainings, because I’m pointing at things and then all I can see is how wrinkly or uneven they look.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess it’s a little silly, maybe, but I came in because I’ve been doing more of the safety trainings at work, and when I’m pointing things out, I keep noticing my hands. They look so wrinkly to me, and uneven, like everybody must be seeing it. I mean, I know hands get older—my mother’s hands were like that too, she worked with them all her life—but mine just seem... I don’t know, wrong somehow.\n\nAnd then there were these newsletter photos from work, and I started thinking my face looked crooked in them. People tell me they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I can’t stop noticing it once I see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about these concerns, like the appearance of your hands or face, for long periods of time, even when you’re trying not to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It sort of gets stuck in my head, especially since I started helping with the safety trainings. I’ll be pointing to something and then I’m looking at my hands instead, thinking how wrinkly and uneven they look. And then later I’ll remember those newsletter photos from work and start thinking my face looks crooked in them, even though people keep saying they don’t see it.\n\nIt’s like I try to move on, but my mind circles back to it.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns about your hands and face bother you a lot emotionally, or make it hard for you to do things you normally would, like participate in meetings or feel comfortable around others?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t really stop me, no… I still go to work, and I still help with the trainings and things. I’ve always been the sort to show up—my dad was like that, never missed a shift unless he absolutely had to.\n\nBut it does slow me down some. Before a training I’ll fuss around longer than I need to, trying to make sure my hands don’t look so bad or that I’m standing a certain way. And I’ve started avoiding photos when I can, like if someone pulls out a camera at work. Not for long, but I’ll turn my face this way and that, like maybe it looks different from one side.\n\nAnd I put lotion on my hands, probably more than I need to. My mother always kept a little bottle in her purse, actually—she’d fuss at us in the winter about cracked hands—but now I’m the one doing it before I have to stand up in front of people.\n\nSometimes I’ll ask a coworker, “Do I look all right?” just sort of casually, though I’m listening pretty hard to the answer. It can make me run a little behind, but I still get where I’m supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other parts of your body or features that you worry about in the same way, or is it mostly your hands and face that you find yourself thinking about?\n\nPatient: Mostly my hands and my face, I think. The hands really started bothering me after I began helping with the safety trainings, because I’m pointing at things and then all I can see is how wrinkly or uneven they look.\n\nAnd then the face thing is more from those newsletter photos at work… I keep thinking it looks crooked, even though people say they don’t see it. I mean, I don’t spend as much time worrying about, say, my legs or anything like that. It’s really those two things that get stuck in my head.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you look at your hands or your face, do you think that the wrinkles or crookedness you see are something that most people wouldn’t notice, or do you feel like they are very obvious and should stand out to others?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess… part of me knows other people say they don’t see it, especially with my face in those newsletter photos. They’ll say, “No, you look fine,” but when I look at it, it just seems crooked to me.

And with my hands, since I started helping with the safety trainings, I can’t seem to stop noticing them when I’m pointing at things. They look wrinkly and uneven, and then I start thinking, well, surely everyone can see that. Although maybe they’re busy looking at the equipment or the signs—I don’t know. My husband used to say people don’t notice half the things we think they do, but it’s hard to believe that in the moment.

It sort of gets stuck in my head, especially since I started helping with the safety trainings. I’ll be pointing to something and then I’m looking at my hands instead, thinking how wrinkly and uneven they look. And then later I’ll remember those newsletter photos from work and start thinking my face looks crooked in them, even though people keep saying they don’t see it.\n\nIt’s like I try to move on, but my mind circles back to it.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns about your hands and face bother you a lot emotionally, or make it hard for you to do things you normally would, like participate in meetings or feel comfortable around others?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t really stop me, no… I still go to work, and I still help with the trainings and things. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… probably it’s things most people don’t really see, or at least they say they don’t. I mean, I came in because since I started helping with the safety trainings, I just can’t stop noticing my hands when I’m pointing things out—how wrinkly and uneven they look.

And then with my face, those newsletter photos at work… I’ll look at them and think it looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it. My sister used to hate every family photo too, she’d always say the lighting was bad—anyway, I guess I know other people may not be noticing it the way I am, but it feels very obvious to me when I’m looking at it.

I don’t know, wrong somehow.\n\nAnd then there were these newsletter photos from work, and I started thinking my face looked crooked in them. People tell me they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I can’t stop noticing it once I see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about these concerns, like the appearance of your hands or face, for long periods of time, even when you’re trying not to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It sort of gets stuck in my head, especially since I started helping with the safety trainings. I’ll be pointing to something and then I’m looking at my hands instead, thinking how wrinkly and uneven they look. And then later I’ll remember those newsletter photos from work and start thinking my face looks crooked in them, even though people keep saying they don’t see it.\n\nIt’s like I try to move on, but my mind circles back to it.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns about your hands and face bother you a lot emotionally, or make it hard for you to do things you normally would, like participate in meetings or feel comfortable around others?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t really stop me, no… I still go to work, and I still help with the trainings and things. I’ve always been the sort to show up—my dad was like that, never missed a shift unless he absolutely had to.\n\nBut it does slow me down some. Before a training I’ll fuss around longer than I need to, trying to make sure my hands don’t look so bad or that I’m standing a certain way. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

The hands really started bothering me after I began helping with the safety trainings, because I’m pointing at things and then all I can see is how wrinkly or uneven they look.\n\nAnd then the face thing is more from those newsletter photos at work… I keep thinking it looks crooked, even though people say they don’t see it. I mean, I don’t spend as much time worrying about, say, my legs or anything like that. It’s really those two things that get stuck in my head.\n\nDoctor: When you look at your hands or your face, do you think that the wrinkles or crookedness you see are something that most people wouldn’t notice, or do you feel like they are very obvious and should stand out to others?\n\nPatient: I guess… part of me knows other people say they don’t see it, especially with my face in those newsletter photos. I mean, I came in because since I started helping with the safety trainings, I just can’t stop noticing my hands when I’m pointing things out—how wrinkly and uneven they look.\n\nAnd then with my face, those newsletter photos at work… I’ll look at them and think it looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It doesn’t stop me from doing things, exactly. I still go to work, and I still help with the safety trainings. I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is.

But I do get… worked up before them sometimes. Like I’ll take longer getting ready, checking my hands or trying to decide if they look bad that day, and then I’m annoyed with myself because I should just be getting in the car. And with the photos, I’ve started sort of stepping out of the way if someone’s taking pictures, or hoping they don’t use one with me in it.

Social things too, a little. I’ll hesitate before going, even if it’s something I used to just enjoy. My neighbor has these little get-togethers, and she makes this dip everyone likes—anyway, I’ll catch myself thinking about whether my face looks off instead of just going and having a nice time. So, yes, it affects my mood some, but I’m still managing.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess it’s a little silly, maybe, but I came in because I’ve been doing more of the safety trainings at work, and when I’m pointing things out, I keep noticing my hands. They look so wrinkly to me, and uneven, like everybody must be seeing it. I mean, I know hands get older—my mother’s hands were like that too, she worked with them all her life—but mine just seem... I don’t know, wrong somehow.\n\nAnd then there were these newsletter photos from work, and I started thinking my face looked crooked in them. People tell me they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I can’t stop noticing it once I see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about these concerns, like the appearance of your hands or face, for long periods of time, even when you’re trying not to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It sort of gets stuck in my head, especially since I started helping with the safety trainings. I’ll be pointing to something and then I’m looking at my hands instead, thinking how wrinkly and uneven they look. And then later I’ll remember those newsletter photos from work and start thinking my face looks crooked in them, even though people keep saying they don’t see it.\n\nIt’s like I try to move on, but my mind circles back to it.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns about your hands and face bother you a lot emotionally, or make it hard for you to do things you normally would, like participate in meetings or feel comfortable around others?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t really stop me, no… I still go to work, and I still help with the trainings and things. I’ve always been the sort to show up—my dad was like that, never missed a shift unless he absolutely had to.\n\nBut it does slow me down some. Before a training I’ll fuss around longer than I need to, trying to make sure my hands don’t look so bad or that I’m standing a certain way. And I’ve started avoiding photos when I can, like if someone pulls out a camera at work. Social things too… I still go, but sometimes I pause and think, “Do I really want to be seen today?” when before I wouldn’t have made such a big deal of it.\n\nDoctor: When you look at your hands or face and notice the wrinkles or the crookedness, what goes through your mind? Do you feel like they are deformed, unattractive, or somehow seriously flawed in a way that others must notice or judge?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t know if I’d say “deformed,” exactly. That sounds so strong. But when I’m up there doing the safety trainings and I point at something, my eyes go right to my hands, and I think, “They look so wrinkly… uneven.” Like that’s what everyone else must be seeing too.\n\nAnd then with my face, those newsletter photos from work just stuck with me. I keep thinking it looks crooked in them, even though people tell me they don’t notice anything. My sister used to say photos never flatter anybody—she hated every school picture of hers—but still, once I see it, I can’t quite unsee it. It feels like there’s something wrong-looking about it, even if maybe other people aren’t seeing it the way I am.\n\nDoctor: When you're in front of others, like during a training or at a social event, do you feel like they are paying a lot of attention to your hands or face, or judging your appearance in a way that makes you feel self-conscious or embarrassed?\n\nPatient: I suppose I do, yes… mostly during those safety trainings. When I’m pointing something out, I suddenly notice my hands and then I think, oh, they look so wrinkly and uneven, and then I start wondering if everyone else is looking at them too.\n\nIt’s funny, I didn’t used to think about it so much before I helped with those trainings. I mean, I’ve worked around machinery and signs and all that for years, but now that I’m up front more, I can’t seem to stop noticing it. And then the newsletter photos… I keep thinking my face looks crooked in them, even though people say they don’t see it. So yes, I get self-conscious, like maybe they’re noticing more than they let on.\n\nDoctor: When you feel self-conscious about your hands or face, do you find yourself doing anything specific, like adjusting your posture, covering your hands, or checking your reflection repeatedly, to try to fix or hide what you're worried about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do a few things, I guess. Before a class or one of those senior-center workshops, I’ll go into the bathroom and check the mirror more than once. Not for long, but I’ll turn my face this way and that, like maybe it looks different from one side.\n\nAnd I put lotion on my hands, probably more than I need to. My mother always kept a little bottle in her purse, actually—she’d fuss at us in the winter about cracked hands—but now I’m the one doing it before I have to stand up in front of people.\n\nSometimes I’ll ask a coworker, “Do I look all right?” just sort of casually, though I’m listening pretty hard to the answer. It can make me run a little behind, but I still get where I’m supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other parts of your body or features that you worry about in the same way, or is it mostly your hands and face that you find yourself thinking about?\n\nPatient: Mostly my hands and my face, I think. The hands really started bothering me after I began helping with the safety trainings, because I’m pointing at things and then all I can see is how wrinkly or uneven they look.\n\nAnd then the face thing is more from those newsletter photos at work… I keep thinking it looks crooked, even though people say they don’t see it. My sister used to hate every family photo too, she’d always say the lighting was bad—anyway, I guess I know other people may not be noticing it the way I am, but it feels very obvious to me when I’m looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns about your hands and face ever make you feel so upset or anxious that it affects your mood, your ability to focus at work, or your enjoyment of social activities?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t stop me from doing things, exactly. I still go to work, and I still help with the safety trainings. I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is.\n\nBut I do get… worked up before them sometimes. Like I’ll take longer getting ready, checking my hands or trying to decide if they look bad that day, and then I’m annoyed with myself because I should just be getting in the car. And with the photos, I’ve started sort of stepping out of the way if someone’s taking pictures, or hoping they don’t use one with me in it.\n\nSocial things too, a little. I’ll hesitate before going, even if it’s something I used to just enjoy. My neighbor has these little get-togethers, and she makes this dip everyone likes—anyway, I’ll catch myself thinking about whether my face looks off instead of just going and having a nice time. So, yes, it affects my mood some, but I’m still managing.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you’re upset or anxious about how your hands or face look, do you feel like you have to do something specific over and over again, like checking your reflection, touching your face, or asking others for reassurance, even if you know it might not help?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I do some of that. Not all day or anything, but before one of the safety classes or those senior-center workshops, I’ll stop in the bathroom and check the mirror a few times. I’ll turn my face this way and that, like somehow the angle will tell me if it looks crooked.

And with my hands, I’ll put lotion on them, sometimes more than once, even if they don’t really need it. My mother always kept hand cream in her purse—funny the things you remember—but for me it’s more like I’m trying to make them look less wrinkly before I have to point at things.

Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker, “Do I look all right?” and then I feel silly because of course what are they supposed to say. It can make me run a little behind, but I still get myself there.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.22, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess it’s a little silly, maybe, but I came in because I’ve been doing more of the safety trainings at work, and when I’m pointing things out, I keep noticing my hands. They look so wrinkly to me, and uneven, like everybody must be seeing it. I mean, I know hands get older—my mother’s hands were like that too, she worked with them all her life—but mine just seem... I don’t know, wrong somehow.\n\nAnd then there were these newsletter photos from work, and I started thinking my face looked crooked in them. People tell me they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I can’t stop noticing it once I see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about these concerns, like the appearance of your hands or face, for long periods of time, even when you’re trying not to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It sort of gets stuck in my head, especially since I started helping with the safety trainings. I’ll be pointing to something and then I’m looking at my hands instead, thinking how wrinkly and uneven they look. And then later I’ll remember those newsletter photos from work and start thinking my face looks crooked in them, even though people keep saying they don’t see it.\n\nIt’s like I try to move on, but my mind circles back to it.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns about your hands and face bother you a lot emotionally, or make it hard for you to do things you normally would, like participate in meetings or feel comfortable around others?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t really stop me, no… I still go to work, and I still help with the trainings and things. I’ve always been the sort to show up—my dad was like that, never missed a shift unless he absolutely had to.\n\nBut it does slow me down some. Before a training I’ll fuss around longer than I need to, trying to make sure my hands don’t look so bad or that I’m standing a certain way. And I’ve started avoiding photos when I can, like if someone pulls out a camera at work. Social things too… I still go, but sometimes I pause and think, “Do I really want to be seen today?” when before I wouldn’t have made such a big deal of it.\n\nDoctor: When you look at your hands or face and notice the wrinkles or the crookedness, what goes through your mind? Do you feel like they are deformed, unattractive, or somehow seriously flawed in a way that others must notice or judge?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t know if I’d say “deformed,” exactly. That sounds so strong. But when I’m up there doing the safety trainings and I point at something, my eyes go right to my hands, and I think, “They look so wrinkly… uneven.” Like that’s what everyone else must be seeing too.\n\nAnd then with my face, those newsletter photos from work just stuck with me. I keep thinking it looks crooked in them, even though people tell me they don’t notice anything. My sister used to say photos never flatter anybody—she hated every school picture of hers—but still, once I see it, I can’t quite unsee it. It feels like there’s something wrong-looking about it, even if maybe other people aren’t seeing it the way I am.\n\nDoctor: When you're in front of others, like during a training or at a social event, do you feel like they are paying a lot of attention to your hands or face, or judging your appearance in a way that makes you feel self-conscious or embarrassed?\n\nPatient: I suppose I do, yes… mostly during those safety trainings. When I’m pointing something out, I suddenly notice my hands and then I think, oh, they look so wrinkly and uneven, and then I start wondering if everyone else is looking at them too.\n\nIt’s funny, I didn’t used to think about it so much before I helped with those trainings. I mean, I’ve worked around machinery and signs and all that for years, but now that I’m up front more, I can’t seem to stop noticing it. And then the newsletter photos… I keep thinking my face looks crooked in them, even though people say they don’t see it. So yes, I get self-conscious, like maybe they’re noticing more than they let on.\n\nDoctor: When you feel self-conscious about your hands or face, do you find yourself doing anything specific, like adjusting your posture, covering your hands, or checking your reflection repeatedly, to try to fix or hide what you're worried about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do a few things, I guess. Before a class or one of those senior-center workshops, I’ll go into the bathroom and check the mirror more than once. Not for long, but I’ll turn my face this way and that, like maybe it looks different from one side.\n\nAnd I put lotion on my hands, probably more than I need to. My mother always kept a little bottle in her purse, actually—she’d fuss at us in the winter about cracked hands—but now I’m the one doing it before I have to stand up in front of people.\n\nSometimes I’ll ask a coworker, “Do I look all right?” just sort of casually, though I’m listening pretty hard to the answer. It can make me run a little behind, but I still get where I’m supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other parts of your body or features that you worry about in the same way, or is it mostly your hands and face that you find yourself thinking about?\n\nPatient: Mostly my hands and my face, I think. The hands really started bothering me after I began helping with the safety trainings, because I’m pointing at things and then all I can see is how wrinkly or uneven they look.\n\nAnd then the face thing is more from those newsletter photos at work… I keep thinking it looks crooked, even though people say they don’t see it. I mean, I don’t spend as much time worrying about, say, my legs or anything like that. It’s really those two things that get stuck in my head.\n\nDoctor: When you look at your hands or your face, do you think that the wrinkles or crookedness you see are something that most people wouldn’t notice, or do you feel like they are very obvious and should stand out to others?\n\nPatient: I guess… part of me knows other people say they don’t see it, especially with my face in those newsletter photos. They’ll say, “No, you look fine,” but when I look at it, it just seems crooked to me.\n\nAnd with my hands, since I started helping with the safety trainings, I can’t seem to stop noticing them when I’m pointing at things. They look wrinkly and uneven, and then I start thinking, well, surely everyone can see that. Although maybe they’re busy looking at the equipment or the signs—I don’t know. My husband used to say people don’t notice half the things we think they do, but it’s hard to believe that in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hands and face are due to defects that are very noticeable to others, or do you worry about things that most people wouldn’t even see?\n\nPatient: I think… probably it’s things most people don’t really see, or at least they say they don’t. I mean, I came in because since I started helping with the safety trainings, I just can’t stop noticing my hands when I’m pointing things out—how wrinkly and uneven they look.\n\nAnd then with my face, those newsletter photos at work… I’ll look at them and think it looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it. My sister used to hate every family photo too, she’d always say the lighting was bad—anyway, I guess I know other people may not be noticing it the way I am, but it feels very obvious to me when I’m looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns about your hands and face ever make you feel so upset or anxious that it affects your mood, your ability to focus at work, or your enjoyment of social activities?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t stop me from doing things, exactly. I still go to work, and I still help with the safety trainings. I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is.\n\nBut I do get… worked up before them sometimes. Like I’ll take longer getting ready, checking my hands or trying to decide if they look bad that day, and then I’m annoyed with myself because I should just be getting in the car. And with the photos, I’ve started sort of stepping out of the way if someone’s taking pictures, or hoping they don’t use one with me in it.\n\nSocial things too, a little. I’ll hesitate before going, even if it’s something I used to just enjoy. My neighbor has these little get-togethers, and she makes this dip everyone likes—anyway, I’ll catch myself thinking about whether my face looks off instead of just going and having a nice time. So, yes, it affects my mood some, but I’m still managing.\n\nDoctor: When you’re upset or anxious about how your hands or face look, do you feel like you have to do something specific over and over again, like checking your reflection, touching your face, or asking others for reassurance, even if you know it might not help?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do some of that. Not all day or anything, but before one of the safety classes or those senior-center workshops, I’ll stop in the bathroom and check the mirror a few times. I’ll turn my face this way and that, like somehow the angle will tell me if it looks crooked.\n\nAnd with my hands, I’ll put lotion on them, sometimes more than once, even if they don’t really need it. My mother always kept hand cream in her purse—funny the things you remember—but for me it’s more like I’m trying to make them look less wrinkly before I have to point at things.\n\nSometimes I’ll ask a coworker, “Do I look all right?” and then I feel silly because of course what are they supposed to say. It can make me run a little behind, but I still get myself there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

My husband used to say people don’t notice half the things we think they do, but it’s hard to believe that in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hands and face are due to defects that are very noticeable to others, or do you worry about things that most people wouldn’t even see?\n\nPatient: I think… probably it’s things most people don’t really see, or at least they say they don’t. I mean, I came in because since I started helping with the safety trainings, I just can’t stop noticing my hands when I’m pointing things out—how wrinkly and uneven they look.\n\nAnd then with my face, those newsletter photos at work… I’ll look at them and think it looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it. My sister used to hate every family photo too, she’d always say the lighting was bad—anyway, I guess I know other people may not be noticing it the way I am, but it feels very obvious to me when I’m looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns about your hands and face ever make you feel so upset or anxious that it affects your mood, your ability to focus at work, or your enjoyment of social activities?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t stop me from doing things, exactly. I still go to work, and I still help with the safety trainings. I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is.\n\nBut I do get… worked up before them sometimes. Like I’ll take longer getting ready, checking my hands or trying to decide if they look bad that day, and then I’m annoyed with myself because I should just be getting in the car. And with the photos, I’ve started sort of stepping out of the way if someone’s taking pictures, or hoping they don’t use one with me in it.\n\nSocial things too, a little. I’ll hesitate before going, even if it’s something I used to just enjoy. My neighbor has these little get-togethers, and she makes this dip everyone likes—anyway, I’ll catch myself thinking about whether my face looks off instead of just going and having a nice time. So, yes, it affects my mood some, but I’m still managing.\n\nDoctor: When you’re upset or anxious about how your hands or face look, do you feel like you have to do something specific over and over again, like checking your reflection, touching your face, or asking others for reassurance, even if you know it might not help?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do some of that. Not all day or anything, but before one of the safety classes or those senior-center workshops, I’ll stop in the bathroom and check the mirror a few times. I’ll turn my face this way and that, like somehow the angle will tell me if it looks crooked.\n\nAnd with my hands, I’ll put lotion on them, sometimes more than once, even if they don’t really need it. My mother always kept hand cream in her purse—funny the things you remember—but for me it’s more like I’m trying to make them look less wrinkly before I have to point at things.\n\nSometimes I’ll ask a coworker, “Do I look all right?” and then I feel silly because of course what are they supposed to say. It can make me run a little behind, but I still get myself there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes, I do. Usually before a class or one of those senior-center workshops, I’ll go into the bathroom and check the mirror a few times. I’ll turn my face different ways, like I’m trying to catch whether it looks uneven or not.

And then my hands—I’ll put lotion on them, sometimes more than once. My mother used to always say dry hands made you look older, which is silly, I know, but it sticks in your head. Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look all right, and then I feel embarrassed because I know I’m probably just bothering them.

It can make me a little late getting going, but I do still get there.

People tell me they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I can’t stop noticing it once I see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about these concerns, like the appearance of your hands or face, for long periods of time, even when you’re trying not to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It sort of gets stuck in my head, especially since I started helping with the safety trainings. I’ll be pointing to something and then I’m looking at my hands instead, thinking how wrinkly and uneven they look. And then later I’ll remember those newsletter photos from work and start thinking my face looks crooked in them, even though people keep saying they don’t see it.\n\nIt’s like I try to move on, but my mind circles back to it.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns about your hands and face bother you a lot emotionally, or make it hard for you to do things you normally would, like participate in meetings or feel comfortable around others?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t really stop me, no… I still go to work, and I still help with the trainings and things. I’ve always been the sort to show up—my dad was like that, never missed a shift unless he absolutely had to.\n\nBut it does slow me down some. Before a training I’ll fuss around longer than I need to, trying to make sure my hands don’t look so bad or that I’m standing a certain way. And I’ve started avoiding photos when I can, like if someone pulls out a camera at work. Social things too… I still go, but sometimes I pause and think, “Do I really want to be seen today?” when before I wouldn’t have made such a big deal of it.\n\nDoctor: When you look at your hands or face and notice the wrinkles or the crookedness, what goes through your mind? Do you feel like they are deformed, unattractive, or somehow seriously flawed in a way that others must notice or judge?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t know if I’d say “deformed,” exactly. That sounds so strong. But when I’m up there doing the safety trainings and I point at something, my eyes go right to my hands, and I think, “They look so wrinkly… uneven.” Like that’s what everyone else must be seeing too.\n\nAnd then with my face, those newsletter photos from work just stuck with me. I keep thinking it looks crooked in them, even though people tell me they don’t notice anything. My sister used to say photos never flatter anybody—she hated every school picture of hers—but still, once I see it, I can’t quite unsee it. It feels like there’s something wrong-looking about it, even if maybe other people aren’t seeing it the way I am.\n\nDoctor: When you're in front of others, like during a training or at a social event, do you feel like they are paying a lot of attention to your hands or face, or judging your appearance in a way that makes you feel self-conscious or embarrassed?\n\nPatient: I suppose I do, yes… mostly during those safety trainings. When I’m pointing something out, I suddenly notice my hands and then I think, oh, they look so wrinkly and uneven, and then I start wondering if everyone else is looking at them too.\n\nIt’s funny, I didn’t used to think about it so much before I helped with those trainings. I mean, I’ve worked around machinery and signs and all that for years, but now that I’m up front more, I can’t seem to stop noticing it. And then the newsletter photos… I keep thinking my face looks crooked in them, even though people say they don’t see it. So yes, I get self-conscious, like maybe they’re noticing more than they let on.\n\nDoctor: When you feel self-conscious about your hands or face, do you find yourself doing anything specific, like adjusting your posture, covering your hands, or checking your reflection repeatedly, to try to fix or hide what you're worried about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do a few things, I guess. Before a class or one of those senior-center workshops, I’ll go into the bathroom and check the mirror more than once. Not for long, but I’ll turn my face this way and that, like maybe it looks different from one side.\n\nAnd I put lotion on my hands, probably more than I need to. My mother always kept a little bottle in her purse, actually—she’d fuss at us in the winter about cracked hands—but now I’m the one doing it before I have to stand up in front of people.\n\nSometimes I’ll ask a coworker, “Do I look all right?” just sort of casually, though I’m listening pretty hard to the answer. It can make me run a little behind, but I still get where I’m supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other parts of your body or features that you worry about in the same way, or is it mostly your hands and face that you find yourself thinking about?\n\nPatient: Mostly my hands and my face, I think. The hands really started bothering me after I began helping with the safety trainings, because I’m pointing at things and then all I can see is how wrinkly or uneven they look.\n\nAnd then the face thing is more from those newsletter photos at work… I keep thinking it looks crooked, even though people say they don’t see it. I mean, I don’t spend as much time worrying about, say, my legs or anything like that. It’s really those two things that get stuck in my head.\n\nDoctor: When you look at your hands or your face, do you think that the wrinkles or crookedness you see are something that most people wouldn’t notice, or do you feel like they are very obvious and should stand out to others?\n\nPatient: I guess… part of me knows other people say they don’t see it, especially with my face in those newsletter photos. They’ll say, “No, you look fine,” but when I look at it, it just seems crooked to me.\n\nAnd with my hands, since I started helping with the safety trainings, I can’t seem to stop noticing them when I’m pointing at things. They look wrinkly and uneven, and then I start thinking, well, surely everyone can see that. Although maybe they’re busy looking at the equipment or the signs—I don’t know. My husband used to say people don’t notice half the things we think they do, but it’s hard to believe that in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hands and face are due to defects that are very noticeable to others, or do you worry about things that most people wouldn’t even see?\n\nPatient: I think… probably it’s things most people don’t really see, or at least they say they don’t. I mean, I came in because since I started helping with the safety trainings, I just can’t stop noticing my hands when I’m pointing things out—how wrinkly and uneven they look.\n\nAnd then with my face, those newsletter photos at work… I’ll look at them and think it looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it. My sister used to hate every family photo too, she’d always say the lighting was bad—anyway, I guess I know other people may not be noticing it the way I am, but it feels very obvious to me when I’m looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns about your hands and face ever make you feel so upset or anxious that it affects your mood, your ability to focus at work, or your enjoyment of social activities?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t stop me from doing things, exactly. I still go to work, and I still help with the safety trainings. I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is.\n\nBut I do get… worked up before them sometimes. Like I’ll take longer getting ready, checking my hands or trying to decide if they look bad that day, and then I’m annoyed with myself because I should just be getting in the car. And with the photos, I’ve started sort of stepping out of the way if someone’s taking pictures, or hoping they don’t use one with me in it.\n\nSocial things too, a little. I’ll hesitate before going, even if it’s something I used to just enjoy. My neighbor has these little get-togethers, and she makes this dip everyone likes—anyway, I’ll catch myself thinking about whether my face looks off instead of just going and having a nice time. So, yes, it affects my mood some, but I’m still managing.\n\nDoctor: When you’re upset or anxious about how your hands or face look, do you feel like you have to do something specific over and over again, like checking your reflection, touching your face, or asking others for reassurance, even if you know it might not help?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do some of that. Not all day or anything, but before one of the safety classes or those senior-center workshops, I’ll stop in the bathroom and check the mirror a few times. I’ll turn my face this way and that, like somehow the angle will tell me if it looks crooked.\n\nAnd with my hands, I’ll put lotion on them, sometimes more than once, even if they don’t really need it. My mother always kept hand cream in her purse—funny the things you remember—but for me it’s more like I’m trying to make them look less wrinkly before I have to point at things.\n\nSometimes I’ll ask a coworker, “Do I look all right?” and then I feel silly because of course what are they supposed to say. It can make me run a little behind, but I still get myself there.\n\nDoctor: When you’re worried about how your hands or face look, do you feel like you have to do something specific — like checking your reflection, adjusting your appearance, or asking someone for reassurance — to feel better or to make sure things look okay?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Usually before a class or one of those senior-center workshops, I’ll go into the bathroom and check the mirror a few times. I’ll turn my face different ways, like I’m trying to catch whether it looks uneven or not.\n\nAnd then my hands—I’ll put lotion on them, sometimes more than once. My mother used to always say dry hands made you look older, which is silly, I know, but it sticks in your head. Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look all right, and then I feel embarrassed because I know I’m probably just bothering them.\n\nIt can make me a little late getting going, but I do still get there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes, it does take up more attention than I’d like. I mean, that’s sort of why I came in. Since I started helping with the safety trainings, I can’t seem to stop noticing my hands when I’m pointing things out—how wrinkly they look, or uneven somehow.

It’s really those two things that get stuck in my head.\n\nDoctor: When you look at your hands or your face, do you think that the wrinkles or crookedness you see are something that most people wouldn’t notice, or do you feel like they are very obvious and should stand out to others?\n\nPatient: I guess… part of me knows other people say they don’t see it, especially with my face in those newsletter photos. They’ll say, “No, you look fine,” but when I look at it, it just seems crooked to me.\n\nAnd with my hands, since I started helping with the safety trainings, I can’t seem to stop noticing them when I’m pointing at things. They look wrinkly and uneven, and then I start thinking, well, surely everyone can see that. Although maybe they’re busy looking at the equipment or the signs—I don’t know. My husband used to say people don’t notice half the things we think they do, but it’s hard to believe that in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hands and face are due to defects that are very noticeable to others, or do you worry about things that most people wouldn’t even see?\n\nPatient: I think… probably it’s things most people don’t really see, or at least they say they don’t. I mean, I came in because since I started helping with the safety trainings, I just can’t stop noticing my hands when I’m pointing things out—how wrinkly and uneven they look.\n\nAnd then with my face, those newsletter photos at work… I’ll look at them and think it looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it. My sister used to hate every family photo too, she’d always say the lighting was bad—anyway, I guess I know other people may not be noticing it the way I am, but it feels very obvious to me when I’m looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns about your hands and face ever make you feel so upset or anxious that it affects your mood, your ability to focus at work, or your enjoyment of social activities?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t stop me from doing things, exactly. I still go to work, and I still help with the safety trainings. I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is.\n\nBut I do get… worked up before them sometimes. Like I’ll take longer getting ready, checking my hands or trying to decide if they look bad that day, and then I’m annoyed with myself because I should just be getting in the car. And with the photos, I’ve started sort of stepping out of the way if someone’s taking pictures, or hoping they don’t use one with me in it.\n\nSocial things too, a little. I’ll hesitate before going, even if it’s something I used to just enjoy. My neighbor has these little get-togethers, and she makes this dip everyone likes—anyway, I’ll catch myself thinking about whether my face looks off instead of just going and having a nice time. So, yes, it affects my mood some, but I’m still managing.\n\nDoctor: When you’re upset or anxious about how your hands or face look, do you feel like you have to do something specific over and over again, like checking your reflection, touching your face, or asking others for reassurance, even if you know it might not help?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do some of that. Not all day or anything, but before one of the safety classes or those senior-center workshops, I’ll stop in the bathroom and check the mirror a few times. I’ll turn my face this way and that, like somehow the angle will tell me if it looks crooked.\n\nAnd with my hands, I’ll put lotion on them, sometimes more than once, even if they don’t really need it. My mother always kept hand cream in her purse—funny the things you remember—but for me it’s more like I’m trying to make them look less wrinkly before I have to point at things.\n\nSometimes I’ll ask a coworker, “Do I look all right?” and then I feel silly because of course what are they supposed to say. It can make me run a little behind, but I still get myself there.\n\nDoctor: When you’re worried about how your hands or face look, do you feel like you have to do something specific — like checking your reflection, adjusting your appearance, or asking someone for reassurance — to feel better or to make sure things look okay?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Usually before a class or one of those senior-center workshops, I’ll go into the bathroom and check the mirror a few times. I’ll turn my face different ways, like I’m trying to catch whether it looks uneven or not.\n\nAnd then my hands—I’ll put lotion on them, sometimes more than once. My mother used to always say dry hands made you look older, which is silly, I know, but it sticks in your head. Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look all right, and then I feel embarrassed because I know I’m probably just bothering them.\n\nIt can make me a little late getting going, but I do still get there.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns about your hands and face feel so important or upsetting that they take up a lot of your attention, even when you’re trying to focus on other things?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does take up more attention than I’d like. I mean, that’s sort of why I came in. Since I started helping with the safety trainings, I can’t seem to stop noticing my hands when I’m pointing things out—how wrinkly they look, or uneven somehow.\n\nAnd then there are the newsletter photos… I’ll see one and start thinking my face looks crooked. Other people say they don’t see it, but once I notice it, it just sort of stays in my head.\n\nIt’s funny, because years ago I wouldn’t have cared about a photo in a newsletter. I’d be more worried about whether my hair was flat or whether I brought enough coffee for the break room. But now I’ll be trying to focus on the training, and part of me is still thinking, are they looking at my hands? Does my face look off? So yes, it pulls my attention more than it should.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I’d say sick, exactly… I mean, I worry it looks bad, or that people notice it, but I’m not usually thinking, “Oh, this must be some terrible physical illness.”

Though, you know, once you get older, you do start wondering about every little thing. My neighbor used to say after sixty you spend half your time checking things that never bothered you before. But with my face and hands, it’s more… I don’t like how they look, and I get stuck on that. Not so much that I think something serious is wrong inside me.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess it’s a little silly, maybe, but I came in because I’ve been doing more of the safety trainings at work, and when I’m pointing things out, I keep noticing my hands. They look so wrinkly to me, and uneven, like everybody must be seeing it. I mean, I know hands get older—my mother’s hands were like that too, she worked with them all her life—but mine just seem... I don’t know, wrong somehow.\n\nAnd then there were these newsletter photos from work, and I started thinking my face looked crooked in them. People tell me they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I can’t stop noticing it once I see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about these concerns, like the appearance of your hands or face, for long periods of time, even when you’re trying not to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It sort of gets stuck in my head, especially since I started helping with the safety trainings. I’ll be pointing to something and then I’m looking at my hands instead, thinking how wrinkly and uneven they look. And then later I’ll remember those newsletter photos from work and start thinking my face looks crooked in them, even though people keep saying they don’t see it.\n\nIt’s like I try to move on, but my mind circles back to it.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns about your hands and face bother you a lot emotionally, or make it hard for you to do things you normally would, like participate in meetings or feel comfortable around others?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t really stop me, no… I still go to work, and I still help with the trainings and things. I’ve always been the sort to show up—my dad was like that, never missed a shift unless he absolutely had to.\n\nBut it does slow me down some. Before a training I’ll fuss around longer than I need to, trying to make sure my hands don’t look so bad or that I’m standing a certain way. And I’ve started avoiding photos when I can, like if someone pulls out a camera at work. Social things too… I still go, but sometimes I pause and think, “Do I really want to be seen today?” when before I wouldn’t have made such a big deal of it.\n\nDoctor: When you look at your hands or face and notice the wrinkles or the crookedness, what goes through your mind? Do you feel like they are deformed, unattractive, or somehow seriously flawed in a way that others must notice or judge?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t know if I’d say “deformed,” exactly. That sounds so strong. But when I’m up there doing the safety trainings and I point at something, my eyes go right to my hands, and I think, “They look so wrinkly… uneven.” Like that’s what everyone else must be seeing too.\n\nAnd then with my face, those newsletter photos from work just stuck with me. I keep thinking it looks crooked in them, even though people tell me they don’t notice anything. My sister used to say photos never flatter anybody—she hated every school picture of hers—but still, once I see it, I can’t quite unsee it. It feels like there’s something wrong-looking about it, even if maybe other people aren’t seeing it the way I am.\n\nDoctor: When you're in front of others, like during a training or at a social event, do you feel like they are paying a lot of attention to your hands or face, or judging your appearance in a way that makes you feel self-conscious or embarrassed?\n\nPatient: I suppose I do, yes… mostly during those safety trainings. When I’m pointing something out, I suddenly notice my hands and then I think, oh, they look so wrinkly and uneven, and then I start wondering if everyone else is looking at them too.\n\nIt’s funny, I didn’t used to think about it so much before I helped with those trainings. I mean, I’ve worked around machinery and signs and all that for years, but now that I’m up front more, I can’t seem to stop noticing it. And then the newsletter photos… I keep thinking my face looks crooked in them, even though people say they don’t see it. So yes, I get self-conscious, like maybe they’re noticing more than they let on.\n\nDoctor: When you feel self-conscious about your hands or face, do you find yourself doing anything specific, like adjusting your posture, covering your hands, or checking your reflection repeatedly, to try to fix or hide what you're worried about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do a few things, I guess. Before a class or one of those senior-center workshops, I’ll go into the bathroom and check the mirror more than once. Not for long, but I’ll turn my face this way and that, like maybe it looks different from one side.\n\nAnd I put lotion on my hands, probably more than I need to. My mother always kept a little bottle in her purse, actually—she’d fuss at us in the winter about cracked hands—but now I’m the one doing it before I have to stand up in front of people.\n\nSometimes I’ll ask a coworker, “Do I look all right?” just sort of casually, though I’m listening pretty hard to the answer. It can make me run a little behind, but I still get where I’m supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other parts of your body or features that you worry about in the same way, or is it mostly your hands and face that you find yourself thinking about?\n\nPatient: Mostly my hands and my face, I think. The hands really started bothering me after I began helping with the safety trainings, because I’m pointing at things and then all I can see is how wrinkly or uneven they look.\n\nAnd then the face thing is more from those newsletter photos at work… I keep thinking it looks crooked, even though people say they don’t see it. I mean, I don’t spend as much time worrying about, say, my legs or anything like that. It’s really those two things that get stuck in my head.\n\nDoctor: When you look at your hands or your face, do you think that the wrinkles or crookedness you see are something that most people wouldn’t notice, or do you feel like they are very obvious and should stand out to others?\n\nPatient: I guess… part of me knows other people say they don’t see it, especially with my face in those newsletter photos. They’ll say, “No, you look fine,” but when I look at it, it just seems crooked to me.\n\nAnd with my hands, since I started helping with the safety trainings, I can’t seem to stop noticing them when I’m pointing at things. They look wrinkly and uneven, and then I start thinking, well, surely everyone can see that. Although maybe they’re busy looking at the equipment or the signs—I don’t know. My husband used to say people don’t notice half the things we think they do, but it’s hard to believe that in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hands and face are due to defects that are very noticeable to others, or do you worry about things that most people wouldn’t even see?\n\nPatient: I think… probably it’s things most people don’t really see, or at least they say they don’t. I mean, I came in because since I started helping with the safety trainings, I just can’t stop noticing my hands when I’m pointing things out—how wrinkly and uneven they look.\n\nAnd then with my face, those newsletter photos at work… I’ll look at them and think it looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it. My sister used to hate every family photo too, she’d always say the lighting was bad—anyway, I guess I know other people may not be noticing it the way I am, but it feels very obvious to me when I’m looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns about your hands and face ever make you feel so upset or anxious that it affects your mood, your ability to focus at work, or your enjoyment of social activities?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t stop me from doing things, exactly. I still go to work, and I still help with the safety trainings. I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is.\n\nBut I do get… worked up before them sometimes. Like I’ll take longer getting ready, checking my hands or trying to decide if they look bad that day, and then I’m annoyed with myself because I should just be getting in the car. And with the photos, I’ve started sort of stepping out of the way if someone’s taking pictures, or hoping they don’t use one with me in it.\n\nSocial things too, a little. I’ll hesitate before going, even if it’s something I used to just enjoy. My neighbor has these little get-togethers, and she makes this dip everyone likes—anyway, I’ll catch myself thinking about whether my face looks off instead of just going and having a nice time. So, yes, it affects my mood some, but I’m still managing.\n\nDoctor: When you’re upset or anxious about how your hands or face look, do you feel like you have to do something specific over and over again, like checking your reflection, touching your face, or asking others for reassurance, even if you know it might not help?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do some of that. Not all day or anything, but before one of the safety classes or those senior-center workshops, I’ll stop in the bathroom and check the mirror a few times. I’ll turn my face this way and that, like somehow the angle will tell me if it looks crooked.\n\nAnd with my hands, I’ll put lotion on them, sometimes more than once, even if they don’t really need it. My mother always kept hand cream in her purse—funny the things you remember—but for me it’s more like I’m trying to make them look less wrinkly before I have to point at things.\n\nSometimes I’ll ask a coworker, “Do I look all right?” and then I feel silly because of course what are they supposed to say. It can make me run a little behind, but I still get myself there.\n\nDoctor: When you’re worried about how your hands or face look, do you feel like you have to do something specific — like checking your reflection, adjusting your appearance, or asking someone for reassurance — to feel better or to make sure things look okay?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I’ll turn my face different ways, like I’m trying to catch whether it looks uneven or not.\n\nAnd then my hands—I’ll put lotion on them, sometimes more than once. My mother used to always say dry hands made you look older, which is silly, I know, but it sticks in your head. Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look all right, and then I feel embarrassed because I know I’m probably just bothering them.\n\nIt can make me a little late getting going, but I do still get there.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns about your hands and face feel so important or upsetting that they take up a lot of your attention, even when you’re trying to focus on other things?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does take up more attention than I’d like. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess… logically I know other people say they don’t really see it. Like with the newsletter photos, I’ll say my face looks crooked, and they’ll tell me, “No, it looks fine.” But to me, once I’ve noticed it, it feels very obvious.

Same with my hands during the safety trainings. When I’m pointing at something, I just see the wrinkles and the unevenness, and I start thinking everyone must be noticing that too. Though, you know, most people are probably looking at the handout or the machine guards, not my hands. My old supervisor used to say nobody pays as much attention to us as we think they do… but it doesn’t feel that way in the moment.
